=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a lowercase English string S of length N and an integer M. For each k=0,1,\ldots,N, solve the following problem:

- There are 26^M lowercase English strings of length M. Among these, find the number, modulo 998244353, of strings whose longest common subsequence with S has length exactly k.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
S

Output

Let \mathrm{ans}_i be the answer for k=i. Print the answers in the following format:
\mathrm{ans}_0 \mathrm{ans}_1 \ldots \mathrm{ans}_N

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq 10
- 1\leq M\leq 100
- N and M are integers.
- S is a lowercase English string of length N.

Sample Input 1

2 2
ab

Sample Output 1

576 99 1

The answers for k=0,1,2 are as follows:

- For k=0: Among length 2 lowercase English strings, those with a longest common subsequence of length 0 with ab include strings such as cd, re, zz, totaling 576.
- For k=1: Among length 2 lowercase English strings, those with a longest common subsequence of length 1 with ab include strings such as ac, wa, ba, totaling 99.
- For k=2: Among length 2 lowercase English strings, there is 1 string (ab) whose longest common subsequence with ab has length 2.

Sample Input 2

3 4
aaa

Sample Output 2

390625 62500 3750 101

Sample Input 3

7 50
atcoder

Sample Output 3

309810541 226923474 392073062 146769908 221445233 435648037 862664208 238437587


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's consider using list dp of length 1<<N. Initialize dp[0]=1. For each of M steps:
  ndp = [0]*states
  for mask, cnt in enumerate(dp):
    if cnt:
      trans = trans_table[mask]
      for c in range(26):
         nm = trans[c]
         ndp[nm] = (ndp[nm] + cnt) % MOD
  dp = ndp

Precompute:
alphabet = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
states = 1 << N
trans = []
for mask in range(states):
    d = {}
    for ch in alphabet:
        old_prev = 0
        new_prev = 0
        nmask = 0
        # maybe use local S and N for speed
        for i, s_ch in enumerate(S, start=1):
            bit = (mask >> (i-1)) & 1
            old_i = old_prev + bit
            if s_ch == ch:
                new_i = old_prev + 1
            else:
                new_i = old_i if old_i >= new_prev else new_prev
            if new_i > new_prev:
                nmask |= 1 << (i-1)
            old_prev = old_i
            new_prev = new_i
        d[nmask] = d.get(nmask, 0) + 1
    trans.append(list(d.items()))

Could optimize by precomputing positions of each char? But N small. Fine.

Potential issue: The loop over alphabet includes chars not in S; for each such char, transition same. But our code recomputes 26 times; still 1024*26*10 = 266k operations, trivial. Precompute time negligible.

            continue

            ndp[nm] = (ndp[nm] + cnt * mult) % MOD

Now, let's consider if there is any hidden requirement to output ans0..ansN for k=0..N, but if N=10, ans length 11. Good.

Let's think about the problem statement: "There are 26^M lowercase English strings of length M. Among these, find the number, modulo 998244353, of strings whose longest common subsequence with S has length exactly k." It says for each k=0,1,...,N. Since LCS length cannot exceed min(N,M). If M < N, some high k impossible; DP will output 0. Constraints M>=1, N<=10, M can be less than N. Example N=10, M=1, ans k>=2 zero. Our DP handles.

Let's test N=10, M=1 with S all distinct. DP after 1 step: mask for char c:
If c in S at position p? Let's compute profile for T=c. v_i = 1 if S[:i] contains c else 0. Since c appears at position p (1-index), for i<p v_i=0, for i>=p v_i=1. Increments d_p=1, others 0. mask has bit p-1. Count for each distinct char in S =1. For chars not in S mask0 count 26-distinct. ans0=26-distinct, ans1=distinct. Good.

Now, let's think about if the DP state should include LCS with S prefixes but not with suffixes? We only need prefixes because recurrence builds T left to right. Yes.

Let's maybe consider an alternative using "subsequence automaton" of S? But no.

Now, let's ensure the recurrence update formula is correct for LCS with S prefix and T+c. Let's prove with an example where match at i but old[i-1]+1 maybe not equal to LCS because there might be a better subsequence not using c? We argued old[i] <= old[i-1]+1, so old[i-1]+1 >= old[i]. But what about LCS(S[:i], T+c) could use c matching some earlier character of S[:i] not the last? Standard recurrence says if last characters match, LCS = LCS(S[:i-1], T)+1. Is it always true? Yes for LCS recurrence when last characters equal: LCS(X+c, Y+c) = LCS(X,Y)+1. But here X=S[:i-1], Y=T, c matches S[i-1] and new char. The standard theorem: If last characters of two strings are equal, then they can be matched in some LCS, so LCS = LCS(prefixes)+1. This is true. But wait, is it possible that matching the last c with an earlier occurrence of c in S yields a longer LCS than matching with S[i-1]? The theorem says if last chars equal, there exists an LCS that matches them, so value = LCS(S[:i-1], T)+1. It doesn't depend on earlier occurrences. Let's test a tricky case: S[:i]="aba", T="ba", c='a'? Actually X="ab", Y="b", c='a', S[:3]="aba", T+c="ba". LCS("aba","ba")=2 ("ba"). old[i-1]=LCS("ab","b")=1, +1=2. Good.
Another: S[:i]="aaa", T="aa", c='a': old[i-1]=LCS("aa","aa")=2, +1=3, LCS("aaa","aaa")=3. ok.
Another: S[:i]="ab", T="a", c='b': old[i-1]=LCS("a","a")=1, +1=2, LCS("ab","ab")=2. ok.
So match formula correct.

For mismatch, recurrence new[i] = max(old[i], new[i-1]). Standard: if last chars differ, LCS = max(LCS(X, Y+c), LCS(X+c, Y)) = max(new[i-1], old[i]). Yes.

Thus update correct.

Now, let's think about if we can reduce state to mask of "frontier" maybe. But current is fine.

Potential issue: The DP over profiles counts strings T of length M, but the profile after processing T is v_i = LCS(S[:i], T). However, the LCS between S and T is v_N. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any problem with modulo and negative? No.

Let's think about the "commit-implement-recheck" instruction. We need output final code only. But before final, we should maybe consider if there is a hidden larger N? Constraints N<=10, M<=100. If N were larger, 2^N impossible. But N=10. Good.

Let's see if there is an even more subtle issue: The number of possible LCS profiles for fixed S is not 2^N? Let's formally define profile p_i = LCS(S[0:i], T) for i=0..N. It satisfies:
- p_0=0
- 0 <= p_i <= i
- p_i <= p_{i+1} <= p_i+1.
Thus increments d_i ∈ {0,1}. So 2^N possible. Yes.

But wait, is p_i <= p_{i+1} always? LCS with longer S prefix is at least LCS with shorter prefix because any common subsequence with S[:i] is also common with S[:i+1]. Yes. And p_{i+1} <= p_i+1 because removing the last char of S[:i+1] can reduce LCS by at most 1? More formally, any subsequence of S[:i+1] either uses S[i] or not; if not, it's subsequence of S[:i], length <= p_i; if uses it, removing it gives subsequence of S[:i] of length <= p_i, so total <= p_i+1. Good.

Thus mask representation complete.

The starter code is Python, likely PyPy3 supports int.bit_count? PyPy 3.10 yes. AtCoder Python 3.11. Good.

Now, let's think about optimizing transition precomputation using bit operations maybe. But current loop is fine. However, let's ensure no bug in transition for masks where old_i and new_prev. Let's write a helper function maybe for clarity.

Pseudo:

def build_transition(mask, ch):
    old_prev = 0
    new_prev = 0
    nmask = 0
    for i, s_ch in enumerate(S, 1):
        old_i = old_prev + ((mask >> (i-1)) & 1)
        if s_ch == ch:
            new_i = old_prev + 1
        else:
            new_i = old_i if old_i > new_prev else new_prev  # max
        if new_i > new_prev:
            nmask |= 1 << (i-1)
        old_prev = old_i
        new_prev = new_i
    return nmask

Note: In mismatch, max(old_i, new_prev). If equal, new_i = new_prev, increment 0. If old_i > new_prev, new_i=old_i. Use `old_i if old_i >= new_prev else new_prev` or `max`. Fine.

Before final, let's thoroughly re-evaluate the DP recurrence and state to avoid a subtle mistake.

Let's formalize:
For a string X (prefix of T), define f_X(i) = LCS(S[0:i], X), i=0..N.
When appending character c to X, the new row f_{Xc}(i) can be computed from f_X:
For i=0: 0.
For i>=1:
If S[i-1] == c:
    f_{Xc}(i) = f_X(i-1) + 1
Else:
    f_{Xc}(i) = max(f_X(i), f_{Xc}(i-1))
This is exactly the standard DP recurrence for LCS when adding one character to the second string. Let's verify with a case where S[i-1]==c but maybe f_X(i) > f_X(i-1)+1? But f_X(i) <= f_X(i-1)+1, so no. Good.

But wait, the standard recurrence for LCS when last characters match is `dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j-1] + 1`, where dp[i][j] = LCS(X[:i], Y[:j]). Here Y[:j] = X_old + c, so Y[:j-1] = X_old. Thus dp[i-1][j-1] = LCS(S[:i-1], X_old) = f_X(i-1). Yes. So match formula correct.

For mismatch, standard `dp[i][j] = max(dp[i-1][j], dp[i][j-1])` = max(f_{Xc}(i-1), f_X(i)). Yes.

Thus state update correct.

Now, let's consider if the profile f_X(i) is enough to compute f_{Xc}(i) for all i. Yes, recurrence uses f_X(i) and f_{Xc}(i-1). It doesn't need f_X for all? It needs f_X(i) and f_X(i-1), so full vector.

Now, let's think about if the number of possible f_X vectors is exactly 2^N. Since f_X(i) is nondecreasing and increments 0/1. But is f_X(i) always <= i? Yes. The binary increment vector d_i = f_X(i)-f_X(i-1) ∈ {0,1}. There are 2^N possible. Good.

Now, let's consider if all binary increment vectors are valid states for some X? Not needed. But if we precompute transitions for all masks, the transition function for an invalid mask might not correspond to any actual LCS row, but it still maps to some mask. Since invalid masks have count zero, no effect. But is it possible that an invalid mask could be reached from a valid mask due to a bug in transition? No, transition from valid produces valid. Let's prove transition preserves the property that there exists some X? Actually if f is a valid LCS row, recurrence yields valid row for X+c. So reachable states remain valid. Good.

Now, let's think about if the transition function computed from a mask that is valid but not necessarily all possible? It uses f_X values. If mask represents f_X, update yields f_{Xc}. Good.

Now, let's maybe write the final code in a clean way:

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353
ALPHA = "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz"

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    M = int(data[1])
    S = data[2]

    states = 1 << N
    trans = []

    for mask in range(states):
        mp = {}
        for ch in ALPHA:
            old_prev = 0
            new_prev = 0
            nmask = 0
            for i, s_ch in enumerate(S, 1):
                old_i = old_prev + ((mask >> (i - 1)) & 1)
                if s_ch == ch:
                    new_i = old_prev + 1
                else:
                    new_i = old_i if old_i >= new_prev else new_prev
                if new_i > new_prev:
                    nmask |= 1 << (i - 1)
                old_prev = old_i
                new_prev = new_i
            mp[nmask] = mp.get(nmask, 0) + 1
        trans.append(list(mp.items()))

    dp = [0] * states
    dp[0] = 1

    for _ in range(M):
        ndp = [0] * states
        for mask, cnt in enumerate(dp):
            if cnt == 0:
                continue
            for nm, mult in trans[mask]:
                ndp[nm] = (ndp[nm] + cnt * mult) % MOD
        dp = ndp

    ans = [0] * (N + 1)
    for mask, cnt in enumerate(dp):
        if cnt:
            k = mask.bit_count()
            ans[k] = (ans[k] + cnt) % MOD

    print(*ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "for mask in range(states):" if states=1024; yes

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353
ALPHA = "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz"

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    N = int(data[0])
    M = int(data[1])
    S = data[2]

    states = 1 << N
    trans = []

    # Precompute transitions for all LCS-profile masks.
    for mask in range(states):
        mp = {}
        for ch in ALPHA:
            old_prev = 0
            new_prev = 0
            nmask = 0

            for i, s_ch in enumerate(S, 1):
                old_i = old_prev + ((mask >> (i - 1)) & 1)

                if s_ch == ch:
                    new_i = old_prev + 1
                else:
                    new_i = old_i if old_i >= new_prev else new_prev

                if new_i > new_prev:
                    nmask |= 1 << (i - 1)

                old_prev = old_i
                new_prev = new_i

            mp[nmask] = mp.get(nmask, 0) + 1

        trans.append(list(mp.items()))

    dp = [0] * states
    dp[0] = 1

    for _ in range(M):
        ndp = [0] * states
        for mask, cnt in enumerate(dp):
            if cnt == 0:
                continue
            for nmask, mult in trans[mask]:
                ndp[nmask] = (ndp[nmask] + cnt * mult) % MOD
        dp = ndp

    ans = [0] * (N + 1)
    for mask, cnt in enumerate(dp):
        if cnt:
            k = mask.bit_count()
            ans[k] = (ans[k] + cnt) % MOD

    print(*ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
